Generalising Projection in Asynchronous Multiparty Session Types
Rupak Majumdar, Madhavan Mukund, Felix Stutz, Damien Zufferey

Abstract
Multiparty session types (MSTs) provide an efficient methodology for specifying and verifying message passing software systems. In the theory of MSTs, a global type specifies the interaction among the roles at the global level. A local specification for each role is generated by projecting from the global type on to the message exchanges it participates in. Whenever a global type can be projected on to each role, the composition of the projections is deadlock free and has exactly the behaviours specified by the global type. The key to the usability of MSTs is the projection operation: a more expressive projection allows more systems to be type-checked but requires a more difficult soundness argument. In this paper, we generalise the standard projection operation in MSTs. This allows us to model and type-check many design patterns in distributed systems, such as load balancing, that are rejected by the standard projection. The key to the new projection is an analysis that tracks causality between messages. Our soundness proof uses novel graph-theoretic techniques from the theory of message-sequence charts. We demonstrate the efficacy of the new projection operation by showing many global types for common patterns that can be projected under our projection but not under the standard projection operation.

1
Introduction

Distributed message-passing systems are both widespread and challenging to design and implement.
A process tries to implement its role in a protocol with only the partial information received through messages.
The unpredictable communication delays mean that messages from different sources can be arbitrarily reordered.
Combining concurrency, asynchrony, and message buffering makes the verification problem
algorithmically undecidable
[
11
]
and principled design and verification
of such systems is an important challenge.

Multiparty Session Types (MSTs)

[
38
,
53
]

provide an appealing type-based approach for formalising and compositionally verifying structured concurrent distributed systems.
They have been successfully applied to
web services
[
58
]
,
distributed algorithms
[
42
]
,
smart contracts
[
24
]
,
operating systems
[
27
]
,
high performance computing
[
35
]
,
timed systems
[
9
]
,
cyber-physical systems
[
48
]
,
etc.
By decomposing the problem of asynchronous verification on to local roles,
MSTs provide a clean and modular approach to the verification of distributed systems (see
the surveys
[
5
,
40
]
).

The key step in MSTs is the
projection
from a
global type
, specifying all possible global message exchanges, to
local types
for each role.
The soundness theorem of MSTs states that every projectable global type is
implementable
:
there is a distributed implementation that is free from communication safety errors such as deadlocks and unexpected messages.

The projection keeps only the operations observable by a given role
and yet maintains the invariant that every choice
can be distinguished in an unambiguous way.
Most current projection operations ensure this invariant by syntactically merging different paths locally for each role.
While these projections are syntactic and efficient, they are also very conservative and disallow many common design patterns in distributed systems.

In this paper, we describe a more general projection for MSTs to address the conservatism of
existing projections.
To motivate our extension, consider a simple load balancing protocol:
a client sends a request to a server and the server forwards the request to one of two workers.
The workers serve the request and directly reply to the client.
(We provide the formal syntax later.)
This common protocol is disallowed by existing MST systems, either because they syntactically disallow
such messages (the
directed choice
restriction that states the sender and recipient must be the same
along every branch of a choice), or because the projection operates only on the global type and disallows
inferred choice.

The key difficulty in projection is to manage the interaction between choice and concurrency in a distributed setting.
Without choice, all roles would just follow one predetermined sequence of send and receive operations.
Introducing choice means a role either decides whom to send which message next,
or reacts to the choices of other roles—even if such choices are not locally visible.
This is only possible when the outcome of every choice propagates unambiguously.
At each point, every role either is agnostic to a prior choice or knows exactly the outcome of the choice,
even though it may only receive information about the choice indirectly through subsequent communication with other roles.
Unfortunately, computing how choice propagates in a system is undecidable in general
[
4
]
;
this is the reason why conservative restrictions are used in practice.

The key insight in our projection operation is to manage the interaction of choice and concurrency via a

message causality analysis
, inspired by the theory of communicating state machines (CSMs) and
message sequence charts (MSCs), that provides a more global view.
We resolve choice based on
available messages
along different branches.
The causality analysis provides more information when merging two paths based on expected messages.

We show that our generalised projection subsumes previous approaches that lift the directed choice restriction
[
17
,
39
,
18
,
41
]
.
Empirically, it allows us to model and verify common distributed programming patterns such as load balancing,
replicated data, and caching—where a server needs to choose between different workers—that are
not in scope of current MSTs, while preserving the efficiency of projection.

We show type soundness for generalised projection.
This generalisation is non-trivial, since soundness depends on subtle arguments about asynchronous messages in the system.
We prove the result using an automata-theoretic approach, also inspired by the theory of MSCs, that argues about traces in
communicating state machines.
Our language-theoretic proof is different from the usual proof-theoretic approaches in soundness proofs of MST systems,
and builds upon technical machinery from the theory of MSCs.

We show empirically that generalised choice is key to modelling several interesting instances in distributed
systems while maintaining the efficiency of more conservative systems.
Our global type specifications go beyond existing examples in the literature of MSTs.

2
Multiparty Session Types with Generalised Choice

In this section, we define global and local types.
We explain how multiparty session types (MSTs) work and present a shortcoming of current MSTs.
Our MSTs overcome this shortcoming by allowing a role to wait for messages coming from different senders.
We define a new projection operation from global to local types:
the projection represents global message exchanges from the perspective of a single role.
The key to the new projection is a generalised
merge
operator that prevents confusion between messages from different senders.

2.1
Global and Local Types

We describe the syntax of global types following work by Honda et al.
[
37
]
, Hu and Yoshida
[
39
]
, and Scalas and Yoshida
[
53
]
.
We focus on the core message-passing aspects of asynchronous MSTs and do not include
features such as delegation.

Definition 1

(Syntax)
.

Global types for MSTs
are defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptμt.\;G\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptt

where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
range over a set of roles
\mathcal{P}
,
m_{i}
over a set of messages
\mathcal{V}
, and
t
over type variables.

Note that our definition of global types extends the standard syntax (see, e.g.,
[
37
]
), which has a

directed choice
restriction, requiring that a sender must send messages to the same role along different branches of a choice.
Our syntax
\sum_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}

allows a sender to send messages to different roles along different branches (as in, e.g.,
[
39
]
).
For readability, we sometimes use the infix operator
+
for choice, instead of
\sum
.
When
|I|=1
, we omit
\sum
.

In a global type, the send and the receive operations of a message exchange are specified atomically.
An expression
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m
represents two events:
a
send

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
and a
receive

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
.
We require the sender and receiver processes to be different:
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
A
choice
(
\sum
) occurs at the sender role.
Each branch of a choice needs to be uniquely distinguishable:
∀i,j∈I.\,i≠j⇒({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j},m_{j})
.
The least fixed point operator encodes loops and we require recursion to be guarded, i.e., in
μt.\,G
, there is at least one message between
μt
and each
t
in
G
. Without loss of generality, we assume that all occurrences of
t
are bound and each bound variable
t
is distinct.
As the recursion is limited to tail recursion, it is memoryless and
generates regular sequences, so a global type can be interpreted as a regular language of message exchanges.

(a)

Load balancing

(b)

Variant of load balancing

(c)

Execution

Figure 1
:
Load balancing and some variant with potential for confusion exemplified by an execution

Example 2

(Load balancing)
.

A simple load balancing scenario can be modelled with the global type:

\mu t.\;\mathsf{Client}\!\to\!\mathsf{Server}\!:\!\mathit{req}.+\begin{cases}\mathsf{Server}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\;t\\
\mathsf{Server}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\;t\end{cases}

The least fixed point operator
μ
encodes a loop in which a client sends a request to a server.
The server then non-deterministically forwards the request to one of two workers.
The chosen worker handles the request and replies to the client.
In this protocol, the server communicates with a different worker in each branch.
Fig.

1(a)
shows this example as a high-level message sequence chart (HMSC).
The timeline of roles is shown with vertical lines and the messages with horizontal arrows.
Different message contents are represented by different styles of arrows.
∎

Next, we define local types, which specify a role’s view of a protocol.

Definition 3

(Local types)
.

The
local types
for a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
are defined as:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.L_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.L_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptμt.L\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptt

where the internal choice
(\IntCh)
and external choice
(\ExtCh)
both respect

∀i,j∈I.\;i≠j⇒({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j},m_{j})
.
As for global types,
we assume every recursion variable is bound, each recursion
operator (
μ
) uses a different identifier
t
,
and we may omit
\IntCh
and
\ExtCh
if
\lvert I\rvert=1
.

Note that a role can send to, resp. receive from, multiple roles in a choice:
we generalise
\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m_{i}.L_{i}
of standard MSTs to
\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i}}!m_{i}.L_{i}
and

\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m_{i}.L_{i}
to
\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i}}?m_{i}.L_{i}
.

Example 4
.

We can give the following local types for
Fig.

1(a)
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathsf{Server}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Client}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Worker_{1}}!\mathit{req}.\,t\;\IntCh\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}!\mathit{req}.\,t}}\right)\\
\mathsf{Client}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Server}!\mathit{req}.\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Worker_{1}}?\mathit{reply}.\,t\;\ExtCh\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}.\,t}}\right)\\
\mathsf{Worker_{i}}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Server}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathsf{Client}!\mathit{reply}.\,t\text{ for }i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}

Note that their structure, i.e., having a loop with at most two options, resembles the one of the global type in
Example

2
.
∎

Our goal is to define a partial
projection
operation from a given global type to a local type for each role.
If the projection is defined, we expect that the type is
implementable
.
We shall show that the global type of
Example

2
projects to the local types in
Example

4
.
As a consequence, the global type is implementable.
Intuitively, when each role in the example executes based on its local type, they agree on a unique global path in an unrolling of the global type.
We formalise projection and soundness in Section
3
.
We note that existing projection operations, including the ones by Hu and Yoshida
[
39
]
as well as Scalas and Yoshida
[
53
]
,
reject the above global type as not implementable.

Notations and Assumptions.

We write
\mathbf{G}
for the global type we try to project.
When traversing the global type
\mathbf{G}
, we use
G
for the current term (which is a subterm of
\mathbf{G}
).
To simplify the notation, we assume that the index
i
of a choice uniquely determines the sender and the message
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}
.
Using this notation, we write
I∩J
to select the set of choices with identical sender and message value and
I\setminus J
to select the alternatives present in
I
but not in
J
.
When looking at send and receive events in a global setting we write
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sending to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

In later definitions, we unfold the recursion in types.
We could get the unfolding through a congruence relation.
However, this requires dealing with infinite structures, which makes some definitions not effective. Instead, we precompute the map from each recursion variable
t
to its unfolding.
For a given global type, let
\mathit{get\mu}
be a function that returns a map from
t
to
G
for each subterm
μt.\,G
.
Recall, each
t
in a type is different.

\mathit{get\mu}
is defined as follows:

\mathit{get\mu}(0)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(t)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(μt.G)\coloneq[t\mapsto G]∪\mathit{get\mu}(G)

\mathit{get\mu}(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i})\coloneq\bigcup_{i∈I}\mathit{get\mu}(G_{i})

We write
\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}
as shorthand for the map returned by
\mathit{get\mu}(\mathbf{G})
.

2.2
Generalised Projection and Merge

We now define a partial
projection
operation
that projects a global type on to each role.
The projection on to a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is a local type and keeps only
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
’s actions.
Intuitively, it gives the “local view” of message exchanges performed by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
While projecting, non-determinism may arise due to choices that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
does not observe directly.
In this case, the different branches are merged using a partial
merge
operator (
\sqcap
).
The merge operator checks that a role, which has not yet learned the outcome of a choice,
only performs actions that are allowed in all possible branches.
The role can perform branch-specific actions after it has received a message that resolves the choice.
For a role that is agnostic to the choice, i.e., behaves the same on all the branches,
the merge allows the role to proceed as if the choice does not exist.

So far, the idea follows standard asynchronous MSTs.
What distinguishes our new projection operator from prior ones (e.g.,
[
39
,
53
]
),
is that we allow a role to learn which branch has been taken through messages received from different senders.
This generalisation is non-trivial.
When limiting the reception to messages from a single role, one can rely on the FIFO order provided by the corresponding channel.
However, messages coming from different sources are only partially ordered.
Thus, unlike previous approaches, our merge operator looks at the result of a
causality analysis
on the
global type to make sure that this partial ordering cannot introduce any confusion.

Example 5

(Intricacies of generalising projection)
.

We demonstrate that a straightforward generalisation of existing projection operators can lead to unsoundness.
Consider a
naive
projection that merges branches with internal choice if they are equal,
and for receives, simply always merges external choices – also from different senders.
In addition, it removes empty loops.
For
Fig.

1(a)
, this naive projection yields the expected local types presented in
Example

4
.
We show that naive projection can be unsound.

Fig.

1(b)
shows a variant of load balancing, for which naive projection yields the following local types:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathsf{Server}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Client}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Worker_{1}}!\mathit{req}.\,t\;\IntCh\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}!\mathit{req}.\,t}}\right)\\
\mathsf{Client}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Server}!\mathit{req}.\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Worker_{1}}?\mathit{reply}.\,\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}.\,t\;\&\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}.\,t}}\right)\\
\mathsf{Worker_{1}}:&μt.\,\mathsf{Server}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathsf{Client}!\mathit{reply}.\,\mathsf{Worker_{2}}!\mathit{req}.\,t\\
\mathsf{Worker_{2}}:&μt.\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathsf{Worker_{1}}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathsf{Client}!\mathit{reply}.\,t\;\&\;\mathsf{Server}?\mathit{req}.\,\mathsf{Client}!\mathit{reply}.\,t}}\right)\end{array}

Unfortunately, the global type is not implementable. The problem is that, for the
\mathit{Client}
, the two messages on its left branch are not causally related.
Consider the execution prefix in
Fig.

1(c)
which is not specified in the global type.
The
\mathsf{Server}
decided to first take the left (
L
) and then the right (
R
) branch.
For
\mathsf{Server}
, the order
LR
is obvious from its events and the same applies for
\mathsf{Worker_{2}}
.
For
\mathsf{Worker_{1}}
, every possible order
R^{*}LR^{*}
is plausible as it does not have events in the
right branch. Since
LR
belongs to the set of plausible orders, there is no confusion. Now, the messages from the two workers to the client are independent and, therefore,
can be received in any order.
If the client receives
\mathit{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}
first, then
its local view is consistent with the choice
RL
as the order of branches.
This can lead to confusion and, thus, execution prefixes which are not specified in the global type.
∎

We shall now define our generalised projection operation.
To identify confusion as above, we keep track of causality between messages.
We determine what messages a role
could
receive at a given point in the global type through an
available messages
analysis.
Tracking causality needs to be done at the level of the global type.
We look for chains of dependent messages and we also need to unfold loops.
Fortunately, since we only check for the presence or absence of some messages, it is sufficient to unfold each recursion at most once.

Projection and Interferences from Independent Messages.

The challenge of projecting a global type lies in resolving the non-determinism introduced by having only the endpoint view.

Example

5
shows that in order to decide if a choice is safe, we need to know which messages can arrive at the same time.
To enable this, we annotate local types with the messages that could be received at that point in the protocol.
We call these
availability annotated local types
and write them as
\mathit{AL}=\langle L,\mathit{Msg}\rangle
where

L
is a local type and

\mathit{Msg}
is a set of messages.
This signifies that when a role has reached
\mathit{AL}
, the messages in
\mathit{Msg}
can be present in the communication channels.
We annotate types using the grammar for local types (
Definition

3
), where each subterm is annotated.
To recover a local type, we erase the annotation, i.e., recursively replace each
\mathit{AL}=\langle L,\mathit{Msg}\rangle
by
L
.
The projection internally uses annotated types.

The projection of
\mathbf{G}
on to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, written
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
,
traverses
\mathbf{G}
to erase the operations that do not involve
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
During this phase, we also compute the messages that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
may receive.
The function
\operatorname{avail}(B,T,G)
computes the set of messages that other roles can send while
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
has not yet learned the outcome of the choice.
This set depends on

B
, the set of
blocked
roles, i.e., the roles which are waiting to receive a message and hence cannot move;

T
, the set of recursion variables we have already visited; and

G
, the subterm in
\mathbf{G}
at which we compute the available messages.
We defer the definition of
\operatorname{avail}(B,T,G)
to later in this section.

Empty Paths Elimination.

When projecting, there may be paths and loops where a role neither sends nor receives a message, e.g., the right loop in
Example

2
for
\mathsf{Worker_{1}}
.
Such paths can be removed during projection.
Even if conceptually simple, the notational overhead impedes understandability of how our message availability analysis is used.
Therefore, we first focus on the message availability analysis and define a projection operation that does not account for empty paths elimination.
After defining the merge operator
\sqcap
, we give the full definition of our generalised projection operation.

Definition 6

(Projection without empty paths elimination)
.

The
projection without empty paths elimination

\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}

of a global type
\mathbf{G}
on to a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}

is an availability annotated local type
inductively defined as:

t{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\langle t,\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},\{t\},\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t))\rangle

0{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\langle 0,∅\rangle

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(μt.G}}\right){\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle μt.(G{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},\{t\},G)\rangle&\text{if }G{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq\langle t,\_\rangle\\
\langle 0,∅\rangle&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}}}\right){\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.(G_{i}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\bigcup_{i∈I}\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},∅,G_{i})\rangle\hfill\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\\[2.84526pt]
\sqcap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\langle\ExtCh_{i\in I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}.(G_{i}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\bigcup_{i∈I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}}\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},∅,G_{i})\rangle\\
\Merge_{i\in I_{[\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}}G_{i}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\end{array}}}\right)\hfill\hskip 9.24994pt\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

sometext

\small\text{ where }I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}\coloneq\{i\in I\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\}\text{ and }I_{[\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}\coloneq\{i\in I\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\}

A global type
\mathbf{G}
is said to be
projectable
if
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is defined for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Projection erases events not relevant to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
by a recursive traversal of the global type;
however, at a choice not involving
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, it has to ensure that
either
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is indifferent to the outcome of the choice or
it indirectly receives enough information to distinguish the outcome.
This is managed by the merge operator
\sqcap
and the use of available messages.
The merge operator takes as arguments a sequence of availability annotated local types.
Our merge operator generalises the full merge by Scalas and Yoshida
[
53
]
.
When faced with choice, it only merges receptions that cannot interfere with each other.
For the sake of clarity, we define only the binary merge.
As the operator is commutative and associative, it generalises to a set of branches
I
.
When
I
is a singleton, the merge just returns that one branch.

Definition 7

(Merge operator
\sqcap
)
.

Let
\langle L₁,\mathit{Msg}₁\rangle
and
\langle L₂,\mathit{Msg}₂\rangle
be availability annotated local types for a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.

\langle L₁,\mathit{Msg}₁\rangle\Merge\,\langle L₂,\mathit{Msg}₂\rangle
is defined by cases, as follows:

•

\langle L₁,\mathit{Msg}₁∪\mathit{Msg}₂\rangle

if
L₁=L₂

•

\langle μt₁.(\mathit{AL}₁\sqcap\mathit{AL}₂[t₂/t₁]),\mathit{Msg}₁∪\mathit{Msg}₂\rangle

if
L₁=μt₁.\mathit{AL}₁\text{, }L₂=μt₂.\mathit{AL}₂

•

\langle\IntCh_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.(\mathit{AL}_{1,i}\sqcap\mathit{AL}_{2,i}),\mathit{Msg}₁∪\mathit{Msg}₂\rangle

if
\begin{cases}L₁=\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{1,i}\text{, }\\
L₂=\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{2,i}\end{cases}

•

\begin{array}[]{llr}\langle&\ExtCh_{i\in I\setminus J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{1,i}&\ExtCh\\
&\ExtCh_{i\in I∩J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.(\mathit{AL}_{1,i}\sqcap\mathit{AL}_{2,i})&\ExtCh\\
&\ExtCh_{i\in J\setminus I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{2,i}&,\\
&\mathit{Msg}₁∪\mathit{Msg}₂\hskip 9.24994pt\rangle\end{array}

if
\begin{cases}L₁=\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{1,i}\text{, }\\
L₂=\ExtCh_{i∈J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.\mathit{AL}_{2,i}\text{, }\\
∀i∈I\setminus J.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}\notin\mathit{Msg}₂\text{, }\\
∀i∈J\setminus I.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}\notin\mathit{Msg}₁\end{cases}

When no condition applies, the merge and, thus, the projection are undefined.
1
1

1

When we use the n-ary notation
\Merge_{i∈I}
and
\lvert I\rvert=0
, we implicitly omit this part.
Note that this can only happen if
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is the receiver among all branches for some choice so there is either another local type to merge with, or the projection is undefined anyway.

The important case of the merge is the external choice.
Here, when a role can potentially receive a message that is unique to a branch, it checks that the message cannot be available in another branch so actually being able to receive this message uniquely determines which branch was taken by the role to choose.
For the other cases, a role can postpone learning the branch as long as the actions on both branches are the same.

Adding Empty Paths Elimination.

The preliminary version of projection requires every role to have at least one event in each branch of a loop and, thus, rejects examples where a role has no event in some loop branch.
Such paths can be eliminated.
However, determining such empty paths cannot be done on the level of the merge operator but only when projecting.
To this end, we introduce an additional parameter
E
for the generalised projection:
E
contains those variables
t
for which
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
has not observed any message send or receive event since
μt
.

Definition 8

(Generalised projection – with empty paths elimination)
.

The
projection

\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}^{E}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}

of a global type
\mathbf{G}
on to a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
is an availability annotated local type
which is inductively defined as follows:

t{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\langle t,\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},\{t\},\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t))\rangle

0{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\langle 0,∅\rangle

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(μt.G}}\right){\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle μt.(G{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E∪\{t\}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},\{t\},G)\rangle&\text{if }G{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E∪\{t\}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq\langle t,\_\rangle\\
\langle 0,∅\rangle&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}}}\right){\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\coloneq\begin{cases}\langle\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.(G_{i}{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}∅}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\bigcup_{i∈I}\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},∅,G_{i})\rangle\hfill\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\\
\sqcap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\langle\ExtCh_{i\in I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}.(G_{i}{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}∅}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}),\bigcup_{i\in I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}}\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\},∅,G_{i})\rangle\\
\Merge_{\,i\in I_{[\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}∧\,∀t∈E.\,G_{i}{\upharpoonright}^{E}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}≠\langle t,\_\rangle}}}G_{i}{\upharpoonright}^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}E}}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\end{array}}}\right)\hfill\hskip 8.50012pt\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

sometext

\small\text{ where }I_{[={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}\coloneq\{i\in I\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\}\text{ and }I_{[\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}]}\coloneq\{i\in I\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\}

Since the merge operator
\Merge
is partial, the projection may be undefined.
We use
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
as shorthand for
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}^{∅}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
and
only consider the generalised projection with empty paths elimination from now on.
A global type
\mathbf{G}
is called
projectable
if
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is defined for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

We highlight the differences for the empty paths elimination.
Recall that
E
contains all recursion variables from which the role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
has not encountered any events.
To guarantee this, for the case of recursion
\mu t.\,G
, the (unique) variable
t
is added to the current set
E
, while the parameter turns to the empty set
\emptyset
as soon as
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
encounters an event.
The previous steps basically constitute the necessary bookkeeping.
The actual elimination is achieved with the condition

∀t∈E.\,G_{i}{\upharpoonright}^{E}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}≠\langle t,\_\rangle

which filters all branches without events of role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.

Other works
[
39
,
18
]
achieve this with
connecting actions
, marking non-empty paths.
Like classical MSTs, we do not include such explicit actions.
Still, we can automatically eliminate such paths in contrast to previous work.

Computing Available Messages.

Finally, the function
\operatorname{avail}
is computed recursively:

\operatorname{avail}(B,T,0)\coloneq∅\\
\operatorname{avail}(B,T,μt.G)\coloneq\operatorname{avail}(B,T∪\{t\},G)\\
\operatorname{avail}(B,T,t)\coloneq\begin{cases}∅&\text{if}~t∈T\\
\operatorname{avail}(B,T∪\{t\},\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t))&\text{if}~t∉T\end{cases}\\
\operatorname{avail}(B,T,\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i})\coloneq\begin{cases}\bigcup_{i∈I,m∈\mathcal{V}}(\operatorname{avail}(B,T,G_{i})\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m\})∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}\}\hskip 8.50012pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∉B\\
\bigcup_{i∈I}\operatorname{avail}(B∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\},T,G_{i})\hskip 8.50012pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∈B\end{cases}

Since all channels are FIFO, we only keep the first possible message in each channel.
The fourth case discards messages not at the head of the channel.

Our projection is different from the one of Scalas and Yoshida
[
53
]
, not just because our syntax is more general.
It also represents a shift in paradigm.
In their work, the full merge works only on local types.
No additional knowledge is required.
This is possible because their type system limits the flexibility of communication.
Since we allow more flexible communication, we need to keep some information, in form of available messages, about the possible global executions for the merge operator.

Merging for
Fig.

1(a)

(b)

Merging for
Fig.

1(b)

some text

Figure 2
:

Availability annotated types for merging on the two examples.
The red lines connect the receptions that get merged during projection.
The annotations only show the receiver’s messages.

Example 9
.

Let us explain how our projection operator catches the problem in
\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\mathit{Client}}
of
Fig.

1(b)
.

Fig.

2
shows the function of available messages during the projection for
Fig.

1(a)
and
Fig.

1(b)
.
In
Fig.

2(a)
, the messages form chains, i.e., except for the role making the choice, a role only sends in reaction to another message.
Therefore, only a single message is available at each reception and the protocol is projectable.
On the other hand, in
Fig.

2(b)
both replies are available and, therefore, the protocol is not projectable.

Here are the details of the projection for
Fig.

2(b)
.
If not needed, we omit the availability annotations for readability.
Recall that
\mathsf{Client}
receives
\mathit{reply}
from
\mathsf{Worker_{2}}
in the left branch, which is also present in the right branch.
Let us denote the two branches as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}G_{1}\coloneq&\mathsf{Server}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\\
&\phantom{\mathsf{Server}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;}\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\;t,\text{ and}\\
G_{2}\coloneq&\mathsf{Server}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\;t\end{array}

Since the first message in
G_{1}
does not involve
\mathsf{Client}
, the projection descends and we compute:

\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{Client}}&=\langle μt.(\langle\mathsf{Worker_{1}}?\mathit{reply}.\,(G^{\prime}_{1}{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{Client}}),\,\operatorname{avail}(\{\mathsf{Client}\},\emptyset,G^{\prime}_{1})\rangle\\
&~\hskip 18.49988pt\sqcap\hskip 0.56905pt\langle\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}.\,(G^{\prime}_{2}{\upharpoonright}_{\mathsf{Client}}),\,\operatorname{avail}(\{\mathsf{Client}\},\emptyset,G^{\prime}_{2})\rangle),\_\rangle\\
\text{where }&G^{\prime}_{1}=\mathsf{Worker_{1}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!:\!\mathit{req}.\;\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\!\to\!\mathsf{Client}\!:\!\mathit{reply}.\;t\text{ and }G^{\prime}_{2}=t.\end{array}

For this, we compute both availability annotations:

\operatorname{avail}(\{\mathsf{Client}\},\emptyset,G^{\prime}_{2})=\emptyset

which is empty as all message exchanges in the recursion (
t
) initiate from
\mathsf{Client}
which is blocked;
and

\operatorname{avail}(\{\mathsf{Client}\},\emptyset,G^{\prime}_{1})=\{\mathsf{Worker_{2}}\triangleleft\mathsf{Worker_{1}}?\mathit{req},\mathsf{Client}\triangleleft\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}\}

which contains both receptions from
G^{\prime}_{1}
only since no receptions from the recursion are added for the same reason.
The conditions are not satisfied as the message for
\mathsf{Client}
in the second branch is available in the first one:

\mathsf{Client}\triangleleft\mathsf{Worker_{2}}?\mathit{reply}\in\operatorname{avail}(\{\mathsf{Client}\},\emptyset,G^{\prime}_{1})
.
Thus, the projection is undefined.
∎

3
Type Soundness

We now show a soundness theorem for generalised projection; roughly, a projectable global type can be implemented by communicating state machines
in a distributed way.
Our proof uses automata-theoretic techniques from the theory of MSCs.
We assume familiarity with the basics of formal languages.

As our running example shows, a protocol implementation often cannot enforce the event ordering specified in the type but only a weaker order.
In this section, we capture both notions through a type language and an execution language.

3.1
Type Languages

A
state machine

A=(Q,\Sigma,\delta,q_{0},F)

consists of
a finite set
Q
of states,
an alphabet
\Sigma
,
a transition relation
\delta\subseteq Q\times(\Sigma\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times Q
,
an initial state
q_{0}\in Q
, and
a set
F\subseteq Q
of final states.
We write
q\xrightarrow{x}q^{\prime}
for
(q,x,q^{\prime})\in\delta
.
We define the runs and traces in the standard way.
A run is maximal if it is infinite or if it ends at a final state.
The
language

\mathcal{L}(A)
is the set of (finite or infinite) maximal traces.
The projection
A{\Downarrow}_{\Delta}
of a state machine is its projection to a sub-alphabet
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
obtained
by replacing all letters in
\Sigma\setminus\Delta
with
\varepsilon
-transitions.
It accepts the language
\mathcal{L}(A){\Downarrow}_{\Delta}=\{w{\Downarrow}_{\Delta}\mid w\in\mathcal{L}(A)\}
.

Definition 10

(Type language for global types)
.

The semantics of a global type
\mathbf{G}
is given as a regular language.
We construct a state machine
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
using an auxiliary state machine
M(\mathbf{G})
.
First, we define
M(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{M(\mathbf{G})},\Sigma_{\mathit{sync}},δ_{M(\mathbf{G})},q_{0M(\mathbf{G})},F_{M(\mathbf{G})})
where

•

Q_{M(\mathbf{G})}
is the set of all syntactic subterms in
\mathbf{G}
together with the term
0
,

•

Σ_{\mathit{sync}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P}\text{ and }m∈\mathcal{V}\}
,

•

δ_{M(\mathbf{G})}
is the smallest set containing

(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i},G_{i})
for each
i∈I
,
as well as
(μt.G^{\prime},ε,G^{\prime})
and
(t,ε,μt.G^{\prime})
for each subterm
\mu t.G^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{G}
,

•

q_{0M(\mathbf{G})}=\mathbf{G}
and

F_{M(\mathbf{G})}=\{0\}
.

Next, we expand each message
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m
into two events,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
followed by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
.
We define
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})},\Sigma_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})},\delta_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})},q_{0\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})},F_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})})
as follows:

•

Q_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}=Q_{M(\mathbf{G})}∪(Q_{M(\mathbf{G})}×Σ_{\mathit{sync}}×Q_{M(\mathbf{G})})
,

•

Σ_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P},m∈\mathcal{V}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P},m∈\mathcal{V}\}
,

•

δ_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}
is the smallest set containing the
transitions

(s,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,(s,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m,s^{\prime}))

and
((s,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m,s^{\prime}),{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m,s^{\prime}))
for each transition
(s,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m,s^{\prime})∈δ_{M(\mathbf{G})}
,

•

q_{0\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}=q_{0{M(\mathbf{G})}}

and

F_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}=F_{M(\mathbf{G})}
.

The
type language

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
of a global type
\mathbf{G}
is given by
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}))
.

Definition 11

(Type language for local types)
.

Given a local type
L
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we construct a state machine
\mathsf{LAut}(L)=(Q,\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},δ,q₀,F)
where

•

Q
is the set of all syntactic subterms in
L
,

•

\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P},m∈\mathcal{V}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P},m∈\mathcal{V}\}
,

•

δ
is the smallest set containing

(\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.L_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i},L_{i})
and

(\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.L_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i},L_{i})
for each
i∈I
,

as well as

(μt.L^{\prime},ε,L^{\prime})

and
(t,ε,μt.L^{\prime})

for each
\mu t.L^{\prime}
in
L
,

•

q₀=L
and

F=\{0\}
if
0
is a subterm of
L
, and empty otherwise.

We define the
type language
of
L
as language of this automaton:

\mathcal{L}(L)=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{LAut}(L))
.

3.2
Implementability

An
implementation
consists of a set of state machines, one per role, communicating with each other through
asynchronous messages and pairwise FIFO channels.
We use communicating state machines (CSMs)
[
11
]
as our formal model.
A CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
consists of a set of state machines
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, one for each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
over the alphabet of message sends and receives.
Communication between machines happens asynchronously through FIFO channels.
The semantics of a CSM is a language
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
of maximal traces over the alphabet of message sends and receives satisfying the FIFO condition
on channels.
A CSM is
deadlock free
if every trace can be extended to a maximal trace.
We omit the (standard) formal definition of CSMs (see
Appendix

A
for details).

Indistinguishability Relation.

In the type language of a global type, every send event is always immediately succeeded by its receive event.
However, in a CSM, other independent events may occur between the send and the receipt and there is no way to force the order specified by the type language.
To capture this phenomenon formally, we define a family of
indistinguishability relations

{\sim_{i}}\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\Sigma^{*}
, for
i\geq 0

and
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}
, as follows.
For all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have
w\sim_{0}w
.
For
i=1
, we define:

(1)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

(2)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.u
.

(3)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\land({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}∨{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}})
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

(4)

If
\lvert w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_}\rvert>\lvert w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}\rvert
,
then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

We refer to the proof of
Lemma

22
in
Appendix

B
for further details on the conditions for swapping events.
Let
w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}
be sequences of events s.t.
w\sim_{1}w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\sim_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
for some
i
.
Then,
w\sim_{i+1}w^{\prime\prime}
.
We define
w\sim u
if
w\sim_{n}u
for some
n
.
It is straightforward that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.
Define
u\preceq_{\sim}v
if there is
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u.w\sim v
.
Observe that
u\sim v
iff

u\preceq_{\sim}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}u
.
To extend
\sim
to infinite words, we follow the approach of Gastin
[
28
]
.
For infinite words
u,v\in\Sigma^{\omega}
, we define
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v

if for each finite prefix
u^{\prime}
of
u
, there is a finite prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
such that

u^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}v^{\prime}
.
Define
u\sim v
iff
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}u
.

We lift the equivalence relation
\sim
on words to languages:

For a language
\Lambda
, we define

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\Lambda)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w^{\prime}\mid\bigvee\begin{array}[]{l}w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}\land∃w∈\Sigma^{*}.\;w\in\Lambda\text{ and }w^{\prime}\sim w\\
w^{\prime}∈\Sigma^{ω}\land\exists w\in\Sigma^{\omega}.\;w\in\Lambda\text{ and }w^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w\end{array}}}\right\}.

For the infinite case, we take the downward closure w.r.t.
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
.
Unlike
[
28
, Definition 2.1]
, our closure operator is asymmetric.
Consider the protocol
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m)^{ω}
.
Since we do not make any fairness assumption on scheduling, we need to include in the closure the execution where only the sender is scheduled, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m)^{ω}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m)^{ω}
.

Example 12

(Indistinguishability relation
\sim
by examples)
.

The four rules for
\sim_{1}
present conditions under which two adjacent events in an execution (prefix) can be swapped.
These conditions are designed such that they characterise possible changes in an execution (prefix) which cannot be recognised by any CSM.
To be precise, if
w
is recognised by some CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and
w^{\prime}\sim_{1}w
holds, then
w^{\prime}
is also recognised by
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
In this example, we illustrate the intuition behind these rules.

For the remainder of this example, the
active role
of an event is the receiver of a receive event and the sender of a send event.
Visually, the active role is always the first role in an event.
In addition, we assume that variables do not alias, i.e., two roles or messages with different names are different.

Two send events (or two receive events) can be swapped if the active roles are distinct because there cannot be any dependency between two such events which do occur next to each other in an execution.
For send events, the 1st rule, thus, admits

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m

\sim_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m

even though the receiver is the same.
In contrast, the corresponding receive events cannot be swapped:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m

\not\sim_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
.
Note that the 1st rule is the only one with which two send events can be swapped while the 2nd rule is the only one for receive events so indeed no rule applies for the last case.

The 3rd rule allows one send and one receive event to be swapped if either both senders or both receivers are different – in addition to the requirement that both active roles are different.
For instance, it admits

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m

\sim_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m
.
However, it does not admit two swap

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m

\not\sim_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.
This is reasonable since the send event could be the one which emits
m
in the corresponding channel.
In this execution prefix, this is in fact the case since there have been no events before, but in general one needs to incorporate the context to understand whether this is the case.
The 4th rule does this and therefore admits swapping the same events when appended to some prefix:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m

\not\sim_{1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.
Then, the FIFO order of channels ensures that the first message will be received first and the 2nd send event can happen after the reception of the 1st message.
∎

Example 13

(Load balancing revisited)
.

Let us consider the execution with confusion in
Fig.

1(c)
.
Compared to a synchronous execution, we need to delay the reception
C\triangleleft W_{1}?\mathit{reply}
to come after the first
C\triangleleft W_{2}?\mathit{reply}
.
Using the 2nd and 3rd cases of
\sim
we can move
C\triangleleft W_{1}?\mathit{reply}
across the communications between the two workers.
Finally, we use the 3rd case again to swap
C\triangleleft W_{1}?\mathit{reply}
and
W_{2}\triangleright C!\mathit{reply}
to get the desired sequence.
∎

This example shows that
\sim
does not change the order of send and receive events of a single role.
Thus, if
w,w^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}^{\infty}
, then
w\sim w^{\prime}
iff
w=w^{\prime}
.
Hence, any language over the message alphabet of a single role is (trivially) closed under
\sim
.
Further, two runs of a CSM on
w
and
w^{\prime}
with
w\sim w^{\prime}
end in the same configuration.

Execution Languages.

For a global type
\mathbf{G}
, the above discussion implies that any implementation
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
can at most achieve that
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
This is why we call
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
the
execution language
of
\mathbf{G}
.
One might also call
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(L))
of a local type
L
an execution language, however, since
\sim
does not swap any events on the same role,
the type language and execution language are equivalent.

Definition 14

(Implementability)
.

A global type
\mathbf{G}
is said to be
implementable
if there exists a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
s.t.
(i) [protocol fidelity]
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
, and
(ii) [deadlock freedom]
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock free.
We say that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathbf{G}
.

3.3
Type Soundness: Projectability implies Implementability

The projection operator preserves the local order of events for every role and does not remove any possible event.
Therefore, we can conclude that, for each role, the projected language of the global type is subsumed by the language of the projection.

Proposition 15
.

For every projectable
\mathbf{G}
, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, run with trace
w
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, there is a run with trace
w
in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.
Therefore, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.

The previous result shows that the projection does not remove behaviours.
Now, we also need to show that it does not add unwanted behaviours.
The main result is the following.

Theorem 16
.

If a global type
\mathbf{G}
is projectable, then

\mathbf{G}
is implementable.

The complete proof can be found in the extended version
Appendix

C
. Here, we give a brief summary of the main ideas.
To show that a projectable global type is implemented by its projections, we need to show that the projection preserves behaviours, does not add behaviours, and is deadlock free.
With
Proposition

15
,
showing that the projections combine to admit at least the behaviour specified by the global protocol is straightforward.
For the converse direction, we establish a property of the executions of the local types with respect to the global type:
all the projections agree on the run taken by the overall system in the global type.
We call this property
control flow agreement
.
Executions that satisfy control flow agreement also satisfy protocol fidelity and are deadlock free.
The formalisation and proof of this property is complicated by the fact that not all roles learn about a choice at the same time.
Some roles can perform actions after the choice has been made and before they learn which branch has been taken.
In the extreme case, a role may not learn at all that a choice happened.
The key to control flow agreement is in the definition of the merge operator.
We can simplify the reasoning to the following two points.

Roles learn choices before performing distinguishing actions.

When faced with two branches with different actions, a role that is not making the choice needs to learn the branch by receiving a message.
This follows from the definition of the merge operator.
Let us call this message the choice message.
Merging branches is only allowed as long as the actions are similar for this role.
When there is a difference between two (or more) branches, an external choice is the only case that allows a role to continue on distinct branches.

Checking available messages ensures no confusion.

From the possible receptions (
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}
) in an external choice, any pair of sender and message is unique among this list for the choice.
This follows from two facts.
First, the projection computes the available messages along the different branches of the choice.
Second, merging uses that information to make sure that the choice message of one branch does not occur in another branch as a message independent of that branch’s choice messages.

Example 17
.

Let us use an example to illustrate why this is non-trivial. Consider:

\mathbf{G}\coloneq\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!l.\,\mu t.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!m.\,t\bigr)+\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!r.\,\mu s.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!m.\,s\bigr)

with its projections:

\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!l.\,\mu t.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m.\,t\bigr)\;\IntCh\;\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!r.\,\mu s.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m.\,s\bigr)\quad\;\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?l.\,0\;\ExtCh\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?r.\,0\quad\;\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\mu t.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m.\,t

and an execution prefix
w
of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!l.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m.

For this execution prefix, we check which runs in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
each role could have pursued.
In this case,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is not directly affected by the choice so it can proceed before the
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has even made the choice.
As the part of the protocol after the choice is a loop, we cannot bound how far some roles can proceed before the choice gets resolved.
∎

Remark 18
.

Our projection balances expressiveness with tractability:
it does not unfold recursion,
i.e., the merge operator never expands a term
μt.G
to obtain the local type (and we only unfold once to obtain the set of available messages).
Recursion variables are only handled by equality. While this restriction may seem arbitrary, unfolding can lead to comparing unbounded sequences of messages and, hence, undecidability
[
4
]
or non-effective constructions
[
17
]
. Our projection guarantees that a role is either agnostic to a choice or receives a choice message in an horizon bounded by the size of the type.

4
Evaluation

We implemented our generalised projection in a prototype tool which is publicly available
[
1
]
.
The core functionality is implemented in about 800 lines of Python3 code.
Our tool takes as input a global type and outputs its projections (if defined).
We run our experiments on a machine with an Intel Xeon E5-2667 v2 CPU.

Table

1
presents the results of our evaluation.

Table 1
:
Projecting MSTs. For each example, we report
the size as the number of nodes in the AST,
the number of roles,
whether it uses our extension,
the time to compute the projections.

]

Our prototype successfully projects global types from the
literature
[
53
]
, in particular Multi-Party Game, OAuth2, Streaming, and two corrected versions of the Instrument Control Protocol.
These existing examples can be projected, but do not require generalised projection.

The Need for Generalised Projection.

The remaining examples exemplify when our generalised projection is needed.
In fact, if some role can receive from different senders along two paths (immediately or after a sequence of same actions), its projection is only defined for the generalised projection operator.
To start with, our generalised projection can project a global type presented by Castagna et al.
[
17
, p. 19]
which is not projectable with their effective projection operator (see
Section

5
for more details).
The Spring-Hibernate example was obtained by translating a UML sequence diagram
[
59
]
to a global type.
There,
Hibernate Session
can receive from two different senders along two paths.
The Group Present example is a variation of the traditional book auction example
[
38
]
and describes a protocol where friends organise a birthday present for someone; in the course of the protocol, some people can be contacted by different people.
The Late Learning example models a protocol where a role submits a request and the server replies either with
reject
or
wait
, however, the last case applies to two branches where the result is served by different roles.
The Load Balancer (
Example

2
) and Logging examples are simple versions of typical communication patterns in distributed computing.
The examples are parameterised by the number of workers, respectively, back-ends that call the logging service, to evaluate the efficiency of projection.
For both, we present one instance (
n=10
) in the table.
All new examples are rejected by previous approaches but shown projectable by our new projection.

Overhead.
