The generalised projection does not incur any overhead for global types that do not need it.
Our implementation computes the sets of available messages lazily, i.e., it is only computed if our message causality analysis is needed.
These sets are only needed when merging receptions from different senders.
We modelled four more parameterised protocols: Mem Cache, Map Reduce, Tree Broadcast, and P2P Broadcast.
We tested these examples, which do not need the generalised projection, up to size 1000 and found that our generalised projection does not add any overhead.
Thus, while the message causality analysis is crucial for our generalised projection operator and hence applicability of MST verification, it does not affect its efficiency.

5
Related Work

Session Types.

MSTs stem from process algebra and they have been proposed for typing communication channels.
The seminal work on binary session types by Honda
[
34
]
identified channel duality as a condition for safe two party communication.
This work was inspired by linear logic
[
31
]
and lead to further studies on the connections between session types and linear logic
[
57
,
14
]
.
Moving from binary to multiparty session types, Honda et al.
[
37
]
identified consistency as the generalisation of duality for the multiparty setting.
The connection between MSTs and linear logic is still ongoing
[
13
,
15
,
16
]
.
While we focus solely on communication primitives, the theory is extended with other features such as delegation
[
36
,
37
,
19
]
and dependent types
[
54
,
26
,
55
]
.
These extensions have their own intricacies and we leave incorporating such features into our generalised projection for future work.

In this paper, we use local types directly as implementations for roles for simplicity.
Subtyping investigates ways to give implementation freedom while preserving the correctness properties.
For further details on subtyping, we refer to work by Lange and Yoshida
[
44
]
, Bravetti et al.
[
12
]
, and
Chen et al.
[
22
,
21
]
.

Generalisations of Choice in MSTs.

Castagna et al.
[
17
]
consider a generalised choice similar to this work.
They present a non-effective general approach for projection, relying on global information, and an algorithmic projection which is limited to local information.
Our projection keeps some global information in the form of message availability and, therefore, handles a broader class of protocols.
For instance, our generalised projection operator can project the following example
[
17
, p. 19]
but their algorithmic version cannot:

\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!a.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!a.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!b.\;0\bigr)+\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!b.\;0\bigr)

Hu and Yoshida
[
39
]
syntactically allow a sender to send to different recipients in global and local types as well as a receiver to receive from different senders in local types.
However, their projection is only defined if a receiver receives messages from a single role.
From our evaluation, all the examples that needs the generalised projection are rejected by their projection.
Recently, Castellani et al.
[
18
]
investigated ways to allow local types to specify receptions from multiple senders for reversible computations but
only in the synchronous setting.
Similarly, for synchronous communication only, Jongmans and Yoshida
[
41
]
discuss generalising choice in MSTs. Because their calculus has an explicit parallel composition, they can emulate some asynchronous communication but their channels have bag semantics
instead of FIFO queues.
The correctness of the projection also computes causality among messages as in our case and shares the idea of annotating local types.

Choreography Automata.

Choreography automata
[
7
]
and graphical choreographies
[
43
]
model protocols as automata with transitions labelled by message exchanges, e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m
.
Barbanera el al.
[
7
]
develop conditions for safely mergeable branches that ensure implementability on synchronous choreography automata.
However, when lifting them to the asynchronous setting, they miss the subtle introduction of partial order for messages from different senders.
Consider the choreography automaton in
Fig.

3(a)
.
It can also be represented as a global type:

+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}m_{1}}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}m_{1}}.\;0\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m_{2}}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m_{2}}.\;0\end{cases}

It is
well-formed
according to their conditions. However,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}
cannot determine which branch was chosen since the messages
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
are not ordered when sent asynchronously.
As a result, it can send
m_{2}
in the top (resp. left) branch which is not specified as well as
m_{1}
in the bottom (resp. right) branch.

start
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}m_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}m_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m_{2}}

(a)

Conditions for choreography automata are unsound in the

asynchronous setting.

(b)

Reconstructible HMSC that is not implementable

Figure 3
:
These examples show that previous results for the asynchronous setting are flawed.

Lange et al.
[
43
]
have shown how to obtain graphical choreographies from CSM executions.
Unfortunately, they cannot fully handle unbounded FIFO channels as their method internally uses Petri nets.
Still, their branching property
[
43
, Def. 3.5]
consists of similar – even though more restrictive – conditions as our MST framework: a single role chooses at each branch but roles have to learn with the first received message or do not commit any action until the branches merge back.
We allow a role to learn later by recursive application of
\sqcap
.

Implementability in Message Sequence Charts.

Projection is studied in hierarchical message sequence charts (HMSCs) as
realisability
.
There, variations of the problem like changing the payload of existing messages or even adding new messages in the protocol are also considered
[
50
,
30
]
.
Here, we focus on implementability without altering the protocol.
HMSCs are a more general model than MSTs and, unsurprisingly, realisability is undecidable
[
29
,
4
]
.
Thus, restricted models have been studied.
Boundedness
[
4
]
is one such example:
checking safe realisability for bounded HMSCs is EXPSPACE-complete
[
46
]
.
Boundedness is a very strong restriction.
Weaker restrictions, as in MSTs, center on choice.
As we explain below, these restrictions are either flawed, overly restrictive, or not effectively checkable.

The first definition of (non-)local choice for HMSCs by Ben-Abdallah and Leue
[
8
]

suffers from severely restrictive assumptions
and only yields finite-state systems.
Given an HMSC specification, research on
implied scenarios
, e.g. by Muccini et al.
[
51
]
, investigates whether there are behaviours which, due to the asynchronous nature of communication, every implementation must allow in addition to the specified ones.
In our setting, an implementable protocol specification must not have any implied scenarios.
Mooij et al.
[
49
]
point out several contradictions of the observations on implied scenarios and non-local choice.
Hence, they propose more variants of non-local choices but allow implied scenarios.
In our setting, this corresponds to allowing roles to follow different branches.

Similar to allowing implied scenarios of specifications, Hélouët
[
32
]
pointed out that non-local choice has been frequently misunderstood as it actually does not ensure implementability but less ambiguity.
Hélouët and Jard proposed the notion of reconstructibility
[
33
]
for a quite restrictive setting:
first, messages need to be unique in the protocol specification and, second, each node in an HMSC is implicitly terminal.
Unfortunately, we show their results are flawed. Consider the HMSC in
Fig.

3(b)
.
(For simplicity, we use the same message identifier in each branch but one can easily index them for uniqueness.)
The same protocol can be represented by the following global type:

\mu t.\;+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}l}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}l}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}l}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}l}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}l}.\;t\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}.\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}.\;t\end{cases}

Because their notion of reconstructibility
[
33
, Def. 12]
only considers loop-free paths, they report
that the HMSC is reconstructible.
However, the HMSC is not implementable.
Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
first chooses to take the top (resp. left) and then the bottom (resp. right) branch.
The message
l
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
can be delayed until after
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
received
r
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}
.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
will first send
r
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and then
l
which contradicts with the order of branches taken.
This counterexample contradicts their result
[
33
, Thm. 15]
and shows that reconstructibility is not sufficient for implementability.

Dan et al.
[
23
]
, improving Baker et al.
[
6
]
, provide
a definition that ensures implementability.
They provide a definition which is based on projected words of the HMSC in contrast to the choices. It is unknown how to check their condition for HMSCs.

CSMs and MSTs.

The connection between MSTs, CSMs, and automata
[
17
,
25
]

came shortly after the introduction of MSTs.
Denielou and Yoshida
[
25
]
use CSMs but they preserve the restrictions on choice from MSTs.
It is well-known that CSMs are Turing-powerful
[
11
]
.
Decidable instances of CSM verification can be obtained
by restricting the communication topology
[
52
,
56
]
or
by altering the semantics of communication, e.g. by making channels
lossy
[
3
]
, half-duplex
[
20
]
, or input-bounded
[
10
]
.
Lange and Yoshida
[
45
]
proposed additional notions that resemble ideas from MSTs.

6
Conclusion

We have presented a generalised projection operator for asynchronous MSTs.
The key challenge lies in the generalisation of the external choice to allow roles to receive from more than one sender.
We provide a new projectability check and a soundness theorem that shows projectability implies implementability.
The key to our results is a message causality analysis and an automata-theoretic soundness proof.
With a prototype implementation, we have demonstrated that our MST framework can project examples from the literature as well as new examples, including typical communication patterns in distributed computing, which were not projectable by previous projection operators.

Appendix A
Communicating State Machines

A
communicating state machine
(CSM)
\mathcal{A}=\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

over
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
consists of a state machine

{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

over
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
A state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
will be denoted by
(Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
If a state
q
has multiple outgoing transitions, all labelled with send actions, then
q
is called an
internal choice
state.
If all the outgoing transitions are labelled with receive actions,
q
is called an
external choice
state.
Otherwise,
q
is a
mixed choice
state.
In this paper, we only consider state machines without mixed choice states.

Intuitively, a CSM represents a set of state machines, one for each role in
\mathcal{P}
,
interacting via message sends and receipts.
We assume that each pair of roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, is connected
by a
message channel
.
A transition
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in the state machine of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
specifies
that, when
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is in the state
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, it sends a message
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and updates its local state to
q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
The message
m
is appended to the channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle
.
Similarly, a transition
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m}q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in the state machine of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}

specifies that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in state
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
can retrieve the message
m
from the head of the channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle
and update
its local state to
q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

Let
\mathsf{Chan}=\{\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\}
denote the set of channels.
The set of global states of the CSM is given by
\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
For a global state
q
, we write
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for the state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
q
.
A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(q,\xi)
, where
q
is a global state and

\xi:\mathsf{Chan}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
maps each channel to the queue of messages currently in the channel.
The initial configuration is
(q_{0},\xi_{\varepsilon})
, where
q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is the initial state of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
and

\xi_{\varepsilon}
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
A configuration
(q,\xi)
is
final
iff
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\xi=\xi_{\varepsilon}
.

In a global move of a CSM, a single role executes a local transition to change its state, while all other roles remain stationary.
For a send or a receive action, the corresponding channel is updated, while all other channels remain unchanged.
Formally, the global transition relation
\rightarrow
on configurations is defined as follows:

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)\cdot m
and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m}(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=m\cdot\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)

and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{\tau}(q^{\prime},\xi)
if

(q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\varepsilon,q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for some role

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and

q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=q^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

A run of the CSM is a finite or infinite sequence:
(q_{0},\xi_{0})\xrightarrow{x_{0}}(q_{1},\xi_{1})\xrightarrow{x_{1}}\ldots,
such that
(q_{0},\xi_{0})
is the initial configuration and for each
i\geq 0
, we have
(q_{i},\xi_{i})\xrightarrow{x_{i}}(q_{i+1},\xi_{i+1})
.
The
trace
of the run, written
\operatorname{trace}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)
, is the finite or infinite word
x_{0}x_{1}\ldots\in\Sigma^{\infty}
.
We also call
x_{0}x_{1}\ldots
an
execution prefix
.
We may omit the subscript
\mathcal{A}
when clear from context.
A run is maximal if it is infinite or if it is finite and ends in a final configuration. A trace is maximal if it is the trace of a maximal run.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
of the CSM
\mathcal{A}
is the set of maximal traces.
A CSM is
deadlock free
if every finite run can be extended to a maximal run.

Notations.

From now on, we fix
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\mathsf{GAut}}(\mathbf{G})
.
Let
w\in\Sigma
.
We write
w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_}
and
w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_}
to denote the projection of
w
onto the send actions from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and receive actions of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, respectively.
We write
\mathcal{V}(w)
to project the send and receive actions in
w
onto their message values.

Equipped with this notation, we define conditions that specify correct executions of a distributed message-passing system.

Definition 19
.

1.

Channel-compliant
:
A word
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
is channel-compliant if messages are received after they are sent and, between two processes,
the reception order is the same as the send order.
Formally, for each prefix
w^{\prime}
of
w
, we require

\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_})
to be a prefix of

\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})
, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

2.

Complete
:
A channel-compliant word
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
is
complete
if it is infinite or the send and receive events match:
if
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, then
\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_})
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

The following lemma summarises that execution prefixes of CSMs satisfy these conditions.

Lemma 20
.

Let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM.
For any run
(q_{0},\xi_{0})\xrightarrow{x_{0}}\cdots\xrightarrow{x_{n}}(q,\xi)
with trace
w=x_{0}\ldots x_{n}
,
it holds that (1)
\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=u

where
\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).u

for every pair of roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}

and
(2)
w
is channel-compliant.
Maximal traces of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are channel-compliant and complete.

Proof.

We prove the claims by induction on an execution prefix
w
.
The base case where
w=\varepsilon
is trivial.
For the induction step, we consider
wx
with the following run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
:

(q_{0},\xi_{0})\xrightarrow{w}(q,\xi)\xrightarrow{x}(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}).

The induction hypothesis holds for
w
and
(q,\xi)
and we prove the claims for
(q^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
and
wx
.
We do a case analysis on
x
.
If
x=\tau
, the claim trivially follows.

Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
.
From the induction hypothesis, we know that
\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=u

where
\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).u
.
Since
x
is a possible transition, we know that
u=m.u^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}
and
\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=u^{\prime}
.
By definition, it holds that

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).m.u^{\prime}=\mathcal{V}((wx){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).u^{\prime}
.
For all other pairs of roles, the induction hypothesis applies since the above projections do not change.
Hence,
wx
is channel-compliant.

Let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.
From the induction hypothesis, we know that
\xi(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=u

where
\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).u
.
Since
x
is a possible transition, we know that
\xi^{\prime}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)=u.m
.
By definition and induction hypothesis, we have:

\mathcal{V}((wx){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_}).m=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}).u.m.

For all other combinations of roles, the induction hypothesis applies since the above projections do not change.
Hence,
wx
is channel-compliant.

From (1) and (2), it follows directly that maximal traces of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
are channel-compliant and complete.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}

Lemma 21
.

Let
L\subseteq\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}^{\infty}
.
Then,
L=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(L)
.

Proof.

For any
w\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}^{\infty}
, none of the rules of
\sim_{1}
applies to
w
, and we have that

w\sim w^{\prime}
iff
w=w^{\prime}
.
Thus,
L=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(L)
for any language
L\subseteq\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}^{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma 22
.

Let
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM.
Then, for every finite
w
with a run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and every
w^{\prime}\sim w
,
w^{\prime}
has a run that ends with the same configuration.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
is closed under
\sim
:
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))
.

Proof.

Let
w
be a finite word with a run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and
w^{\prime}\sim w
.
By definition,
w^{\prime}\sim_{n}w
for some
n
.
We prove that
w^{\prime}
has a run that ends in the same configuration by induction on
n
.
The base case for
n=0
is trivial.
For the induction step, we assume that the claim holds for
n
and prove it for
n+1
.
Suppose that
w\sim_{n+1}w^{\prime}
.
Then, there is
w^{\prime\prime}
such that
w^{\prime}\sim_{1}w^{\prime\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}\sim_{n}w
.
By assumption, we know that
w^{\prime}=u^{\prime}u_{1}u_{2}u^{\prime\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}=u^{\prime}u_{2}u_{1}u^{\prime\prime}
for some
u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}
,
u_{1},u_{2}\in\Sigma
.
By induction hypothesis, we know that
w^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
so there is run for
w^{\prime\prime}
in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Let us investigate the run at
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
:

\cdots(q_{1},\xi_{1})\xrightarrow{u_{1}}(q_{2},\xi_{2})\xrightarrow{u_{2}}(q_{3},\xi_{3})\cdots.

It suffices to show that

\cdots(q_{1},\xi_{1})\xrightarrow{u_{2}}(q^{\prime}_{2},\xi^{\prime}_{2})\xrightarrow{u_{1}}(q_{3},\xi_{3})\cdots

is possible in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
for some configuration
(q^{\prime}_{2},\xi^{\prime}_{2})
.
We do a case analysis on the rule that was applied for
w^{\prime}\sim_{1}w^{\prime\prime}
.

•

u_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
,

u_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m^{\prime}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
:

We define
q^{\prime}_{2}
such that

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=q_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
,
and

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Both transitions are feasible in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
because both
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
are different and send a message to different channels.
They can do this independently from each other.

•

u_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
,

u_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
:

We define
q^{\prime}_{2}
such that

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}=q_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}
,
and

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Both transitions are feasible in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
because both
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
are different and receive a message from a different channel.
They can do this independently from each other.

•

u_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
,

u_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}
, and
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\land({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}∨{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}})
.

We define
q^{\prime}_{2}
such that

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=q_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}}
,
and

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
Let us do a case split according to the side conditions.
First, let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
.
The channels of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are different and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
are different, so both transitions are feasible in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Second, let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
.
The channels of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are different and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
are different, so both transitions are feasible in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

•

u_{1}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
,

u_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m^{\prime}
,
and
\lvert u^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_}\rvert>\lvert u^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}\rvert
:

We define
q^{\prime}_{2}
such that

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=q_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,
and

q^{\prime}_{2,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}=q_{3,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}}

for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

In this case, the channel of
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
is the same but the side condition ensures that
u_{2}
actually has a different message read since the channel
\xi_{1}(\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle)
is not empty by

Lemma

20

and, hence, both transitions can act independently and lead to the same configuration.

This proves that
w^{\prime}
has a run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
that ends in the same configuration which concludes the proof of the first claim.

For the second claim, we know that
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))
by definition.
Hence, it suffices to show that

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

We show the claim for finite traces first:

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))\cap\Sigma^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\cap\Sigma^{*}.

Let
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))\;\cap\;\Sigma^{*}
.
There is
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\;\cap\;\Sigma^{*}
such that
w\sim w^{\prime}
.
By definition,
w
has a run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
which ends in a final configuration.
From the first claim, we know that
w^{\prime}
also has a run that ends in the same configuration which is final.
Therefore,
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\cap\Sigma^{*}
.
Hence, the claim holds for finite traces.

It remains to show the claim for infinite traces.
To this end, we show that for every execution prefix
w
of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
w\sim u
for
u\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))
and any continuation
x
of
w
, i.e.,
wx
is an execution prefix of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, it holds that
wx\sim ux
and
ux\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))

(\square)
.
We know that
w\sim_{n}u
for some
n
by definition, so
wx\sim_{n}ux
since we can mimic the same
n
swaps when extending both
w
and
u
by
x
.
From the first claim, we know that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is in the same configuration
(q,\xi)
after processing
w
and
u
.
Therefore,
ux
is an execution prefix of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
because
wx
is which yields
(\square)
.

We show that

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\cap\Sigma^{\omega}\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\cap\Sigma^{\omega}.

Let
w\in\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\,\cap\,\Sigma^{\omega}
.
We show that
w
has an infinite run in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Consider a tree
\mathcal{T}
where each node corresponds to a run
\rho
on some finite prefix
w^{\prime}\leq w
in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
The root is labelled by the empty run.
The children of a node
\rho
are runs that extend
\rho
by a single transition — these exist by
(\square)
.
Since our CSM, derived from a global type, is built from a finite number of finite state machines,
\mathcal{T}
is finitely branching.
By König’s Lemma, there is an infinite path in
\mathcal{T}
that corresponds to an infinite run for
w
in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, so
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\cap\Sigma^{\omega}
.
∎

Lemma 23
.

Let
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
be channel-compliant.
Then,
w\sim w^{\prime}

iff
w^{\prime}
is channel-compliant and

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for all roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

We use the characterisation of
\sim
using dependence graphs
[
28
]
.
For a word
w
and a letter
a\in\Sigma
that appears in
w
, let

(a,i)
denote the
i
th occurrence of
a
in
w
. Define the dependence graph
(V,E,\lambda)
, where

V=\{(a,i)\mid a\in\Sigma,i\geq 1\}
,

E=\{((a,i),(b,j))\mid\mbox{$a$ and $b$ cannot be swapped and
$(a,i)$ occurs before $(b,j)$ in $w$}\}
,
and

\lambda(a,i)=a
for all
a\in\Sigma
,
i\geq 1
.
A fundamental result of trace theory states that
w\sim w^{\prime}
iff they have isomorphic dependence graphs.
We observe that for two channel compliant words, the ordering of the letters on each
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}

ensures isomorphic dependence graphs, since the ordering of receives is thus fixed.
∎

Appendix C
Implementing Projectable Types: Proofs

Let us start with some structural properties of
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
and
\mathsf{LAut}(L)
.

Proposition 24

(Shape of
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
for global type
G
and
\mathsf{LAut}(L)
for local type
L
)
.

Let
G
be a global type and
L
a local type.
Then, the following holds:

1.

every state
q
in
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
or
\mathsf{LAut}(L)
either only has non-
\varepsilon
-transitions or a single
\varepsilon
-transition;

2.

the state that corresponds to the syntactic subterm
0
, if it exists, is the only state without any outgoing transitions.

3.

for each word
w
, there are at most three runs
\rho
,
\rho_{1}
, and
\rho_{2}
in
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
; moreover, when they exist,
the runs can be chosen such that
\rho_{1}:=\rho\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q
and
\rho_{2}:=\rho_{1}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}q^{\prime}
for some
q
,
q^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Fact (a) follows from the definition of
\mathsf{LAut}(L)
and
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
and the syntax restriction that every variable is only bound once.
Fact (b) also follows directly from the definition of
\mathsf{LAut}(L)
and
\mathsf{GAut}(G)
.
Let us prove Fact (c).
For
\mathsf{LAut}(G)
branches occur for sends only.
By definition of the syntax for global types, these send options are always distinct.
Hence, the runs are unique up to
\varepsilon
-transitions.
Such
\varepsilon
-transitions can only occur in the presence of recursion.
By assumption, recursion is always guarded and hence the number of consecutive
\varepsilon
-transitions is bounded by
2
.
∎

C.1
Projection Preserves Behaviours

Lemma 25
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type.
Then, the following holds:

1.

for every prefix
w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
, there is a run
\rho
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and for every extension
wx
of
w
in
\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
,
\rho
can be extended

2.

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

Proof.

For (1), let
w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
We prove the claim by induction on the length of
w
.

The base case,
w=\varepsilon
, is trivial.
For the induction step, we assume that the claim holds for
w
with run
\rho
and we extend
\rho
for
wx\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
We do a case analysis on
x
.

First, suppose that
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and
m
.
By
Proposition

15
, we know that
(wx){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
has a run in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Therefore, we can simply extend the run
\rho
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Second, suppose that
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and
m
.
Again, by
Proposition

15
, we know that
(wx){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
has a run in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.
Any prefix of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
is channel-compliant by construction. From
Lemma

20
, we know that
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\rangle
is of the form
m\cdot u
. Therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
can receive
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and we can extend
\rho
for
wx
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.

In both cases, we have extended
\rho
on
w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
to
wx\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
, completing the argument for (1).

For (2), let
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
We do a case analysis whether
w
is finite or infinite.

For finite words
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\cap\Sigma^{*}
, we know that
w
is channel-compliant and complete.
(1) tells us that there is a run for
w
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
that reaches
(q,\xi)
for some state
q
and channel evaluation
\xi
.
We need to show that
(q,\xi)
is a final configuration, i.e. (a)
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is a final state in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
and (b) all channels in
\xi
are empty.

To establish (b), we observe that all channels are empty after a run of a finite and complete word
w
, by
Lemma

20
. The fact that
w
is complete follows from
Definition

10
.

For (a), recall that
w\in\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
and
w
is finite, so the run with trace
w
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
ends in a final state.
By
Proposition

24
, every final state corresponds to
0
and has no outgoing transitions.
The projection of
0{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is defined as
0
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
0
is never merged with local types different from
0
.
The definition of local types prescribes that all different send and reception options are distinct at all times.
This implies that
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
is deterministic (if it exists which is given by assumption).
Therefore, each
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
corresponds to the local type
0
and is hence final in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Thus,
(q,\xi)
is a final configuration of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, so
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

Suppose that
w
is infinite, i.e.,
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\cap\Sigma^{\omega}
.
We show that
w
has an infinite run in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Consider a tree
\mathcal{T}
where each node corresponds to a run
\rho
on some finite prefix
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
The root is labelled by the empty run. The children of a node
\rho
are runs that extend
\rho
by a single transition—these exist by part (1) of the lemma.
Since our CSM, derived from a global type, is a finite number of finite state machines,
\mathcal{T}
is finitely branching.
By König’s Lemma, there is an infinite path in
\mathcal{T}
that corresponds
to an infinite run for
w
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, so
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.
∎

C.2
Projection does Not Introduce New Behaviours

Definition 26

(Run mappings)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM.
For an execution prefix
w
of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
and a run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that
w
is a prefix of some
w^{\prime}
with
w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}(\rho)
,
a
run mapping

\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}\colon\mathcal{P}\to\operatorname{pref}(\rho)
is a mapping such that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}(\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.

We say that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a
family of run mappings
for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})

iff
for every execution prefix
w
of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
,
there is a
witness run

\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
with a run mapping
\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}
.

When clear from context, we omit the subscript of run mappings
\rho_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}(\hbox{-})
.

Given a global type
\mathbf{G}
and an execution prefix of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, a run mapping guarantees that there is some common run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that all roles could have pursued when observing this execution prefix.
They might not have processed all their events along this run, but they have not diverged.
A family of run mappings lifts this to all execution prefixes.
However, this does not yet ensure that every role will be able to follow this run to the end.
This will be part of
Control Flow Agreement
.

Lemma 27

(
\{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a family of run mappings)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type.
Then,
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a family of run mappings for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.

The proof is not trivial because the merge can collapse different branches for a role so it follows sets of runs.
The intersection of all these sets contains (a prefix of) the witness run.
In some case, e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
in the previous example, a role can “overtake” a choice and perform actions that belong to the branches of a choice before the choosing process has made the choice.

Outline and Motivation of Concepts

As outlined in the main text, the overall proof works by induction on the execution prefix.
While the skeleton is quite basic, let us give an intuition which properties are needed for the cases where some role receives or sends a message in the extension of an execution prefix.
Based on these properties, we can explain the different concepts which need to be formalised for these properties.
In both cases, we need to argue that the previous run mapping can be kept for all roles but the one that takes a step.

For the receive case, suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
receives from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
On the one hand, we need to argue that the run for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can actually be extended to match the one of of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
On the other hand, we also need to ensure that there is no other message that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
could receive so that it follows a run different from the one for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
While the first is easy to obtain by the definition of projection, we need to argue that the message availability check does indeed compute the available messages when
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has not proceeded with any other event yet.

For the send case, suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and chooses between different branches, i.e., there actually is a choice between different continuations.
Though, there might be roles with (some) actions that do not depend on this choice and we have to ensure that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s run can be extended to some prefix of theirs.
For this, we exploit the idea of prophecy variables and assume that all of those roles took the branch
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
will choose but we need to ensure that this does not restrain them in any way.
So intuitively for each of these roles, we need to compare the executions along the different branches
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can choose from and need to prove that they are the same if
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has not yet committed this choice yet.
(Note the subtlety that only the part of the execution which does not depend on this choice needs to be the same.)

To formalise these ideas, we introduce three concepts.

•

Blocked languages:

We inductively define the (global) language
L_{(G\ldots)}^{\mathcal{B}}
that captures all executions that are possible from subterm
G
in some global type
\mathbf{G}
, under the assumption that no role in
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
can take any further step.
We might call these roles
blocked
.
This definition serves for the formalisation of semantic arguments about the availability of messages and the languages along different choice branches.

•

Blocked projection operator:

We introduce a variation of the projection operator that also allows to account for a set of blocked roles
\mathcal{B}
, prove its connection to the above languages, and use the relation to the standard projection operator to show the equality of the “blocked” languages along different choices.

•

Extended local types:

In contrast to the standard projection operator, the blocked one can declare intermediate syntactic subterms as final states.
Standard local types do not allow this so we introduce extended local types.

Blocked Languages – The Language-theoretic Perspective

We will use blocked languages to show that no role can proceed with an action that is specific to some choice which has not been committed yet.

Definition 28

(Blocked language along path)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
a set of roles. Then,
L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
is inductively defined:

L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(0\ldots)}\coloneq\{\varepsilon\}

L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(t\ldots)}\coloneq L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\mu t.G\ldots)}

L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\mu t.G\ldots)}\coloneq L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}

L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\Sigma_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}\ldots)}\coloneq\bigcup_{i\in I}\{f(\mathcal{B},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}).w\mid w\in L^{g(\mathcal{B},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i})}_{(G_{i}\ldots)}\}

where

f(\mathcal{B},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i})=\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}\land{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\notin\mathcal{B}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}\land{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\in\mathcal{B}\\
\varepsilon&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

g(\mathcal{B},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{B}&\text{if }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{B}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Based on this definition, we compute the set of messages that can occur when some roles, i.e, the ones in
\mathcal{B}
are blocked:

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}\coloneq\bigcup_{x=x_{1}\ldots\in L^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}}\{x_{i}\mid x_{i}\in\Sigma_{r}\}.

Note that we did not use the intermediate states but only the ones from
M(\mathbf{G})
in this definition for clarity but mimic splitting the message exchanges faithfully.
We implicitly remove
\varepsilon
, however, they are important so that languages are defined.

Extended Local Types and Blocked Projection Operator –

The Type-theoretic Perspective

Definition 29

(Extended local types)
.

We extend local types with marks
\ast
or
\circ
for syntactic subterms and obtain
extended local types
with the following inductive rules where
\circledast\in\{\circ,\ast\}
:

\displaystyle\mathit{\mathit{EL}}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq[0,\circledast]\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt[\IntCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}.\mathit{EL}_{i},\circledast]\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt[\ExtCh_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}?m_{i}.\mathit{EL}_{i},\circledast]\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt[μt.\mathit{EL},\circledast]\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt[t,\circledast]

Extended local types can be annotated with availability information about messages as local types.
When working with message set annotations, we assume to have extended availability annotated local types of shape
[\langle\hbox{-},\hbox{-}\rangle,\hbox{-}]
.
Thus, we can re-use the merge-operator
\sqcap
.

The mark
\ast
indicates that this syntactic subterm shall be considered final in the semantics of the extended local types.
Any syntactic subterm
[\mathit{EL}^{\prime},\circledast^{\prime}]
of
[EL,\circledast]
has a syntax tree.
Intuitively,
\circledast^{\prime}
marks the root of the syntax tree of
\mathit{EL}^{\prime}
and tells whether one needs to continue, i.e.
[EL^{\prime},\circ]
, to obtain an accepting word or may stop, i.e.
[EL^{\prime},\ast]
, at this point.
Without such an extension, there is no way to specify such behaviour in local types (and global type) since
0
explicitly marks the end of the type.
