requires that both sets
I
and
J
are the same.
Since
\circledast_{i}=\circledast_{j}
, there needs to be some message exchange in one branch that cannot happen in the other branch.
However, analogous to the previous case, the send event of such a reception event must not depend on the choice of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
which branch to take.
However, this is not possible with the blocked projection operator so
\sqcap
would also be undefined in this case which yields a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 37

(Property of Projectable global types II - receive)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be some global type and
G
be some syntactic subterm of
\mathbf{G}
.
It holds that

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}\subseteq\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},∅,G)
for every
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Let
T^{\prime}
be all recursion variables in
G
that are not bound in
G
.
We define
\widehat{G}
to be the type that is obtained by substituting every
t\in T^{\prime}
by
\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t)
.
By construction,
\widehat{G}
is a global type (and not only a syntactic subterm of a latter).

Let
T
be the set of all recursion variables in
\widehat{G}
.
Since we unfolded the unbound recursion variables up-front in
\widehat{G}
prohibits us from substituting for any recursion variable again,
it is straightforward, from the definition of
\operatorname{avail}(\hbox{-},\hbox{-},\hbox{-})
, that the following holds:

\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},\emptyset,G)=\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})

By construction of
\widehat{G}
, the computation of
\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})
will never apply the second case which is the only one where we do not simply descend in the structure of the third parameter so induction hypotheses will apply.
By the equi-recursive view on global types, it holds that

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}=M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G}\ldots)}

and therefore it suffices to show that

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G}\ldots)}\subseteq\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G}).

We do induction on the structure of
\widehat{G}
to prove the claim.
For the base case where
\widehat{G}=0
, the claim follows trivially.
For
\widehat{G}=t
, we know that either
t\in T^{\prime}
has been substituted by its definition using
\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(\hbox{-})
or it was bound in
G
.
Normally, one would need to substitute
t
again and consider the messages.
However, in both cases, we know that we traversed all branches from the binder to the variable at least once and unfolding further does not change the set of available messages.

For the induction step, suppose that
\widehat{G}=\mu t.\widehat{G^{\prime}}
.
By induction hypothesis, we know that the claim holds for
\widehat{G^{\prime}}
and the binder for recursion variable
t
does not add any message to the set of messages:

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\mu t.\widehat{G^{\prime}}\ldots)}=M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G^{\prime}}\ldots)}.

By definition, we have that

\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\mu t.\widehat{G^{\prime}})=\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T∪\{t\},\widehat{G^{\prime}})=\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G^{\prime}}).

By induction hypothesis, it holds that

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G^{\prime}}\ldots)}\subseteq\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G^{\prime}})

which proves the claim.

Last, let

\widehat{G}=\Sigma_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}

for some index set
I
, roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
, message values
m_{i}
and global subterms
\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}
for every
i\in I
.
We do a case analysis on whether
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}
.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{B}
.
Then, all receipts are added by definition of
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{\_}
:

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G}\ldots)}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}\mid i\in I\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}.\ldots)}.

For
\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})
, the fourth case in the definition applies:

\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m_{i}\mid i∈I\}∪\bigcup_{i∈I}\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}).

The first part of the equations are identical and combining the induction hypotheses for each
\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}
proves the claim.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{B}
.
Then, by definition, it holds that that
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G}\ldots)}
does not contain any of the receipts
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_
.
Additionally,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
is added to
\mathcal{B}
by definition:

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(\widehat{G}\ldots)}=\bigcup_{i\in I}M^{\mathcal{B}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\}}_{(\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}.\ldots)}.

For
\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})
, the fifth case applies:

\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},T,\widehat{G})=\bigcup_{i∈I}\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\},T,\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}).

Combining the induction hypotheses for each
\widehat{G^{\prime}_{i}}
proves the claim.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

27
:

Existence of Family of Run Mappings for Projectable Global Types

Equipped with these properties, we can prove
Lemma

27
which reads:
Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type.
Then,
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a family of run mappings for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.

Proof.

Let
w
be a prefix of an execution of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
We prove the claim by induction on the length of
w
.
The base case where
w=\varepsilon
is trivial:
\rho=\varepsilon
and
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\varepsilon
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

For the induction step, we append
x
to obtain
wx
for which
x=\tau
,

x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
or

x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
m
.
As induction hypothesis, we assume that there is some run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma}
is the prefix of
w^{\prime}
with

w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)

and a run mapping
\rho(\hbox{-})
such that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

For all cases, we re-use the witness run
\rho
by extending it to
\rho^{\prime}
when necessary and re-use the run mapping
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
except for at most one process.

First
, Suppose that
x=\tau
.
Then, the claim follows with the same run
\rho
and mapping
\rho(\hbox{-})
.
It is straightforward that
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=(w\tau){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
holds.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be the process which takes an
\varepsilon
-transition and hence leads to the extension by
\tau
.
By
Proposition

24
, we know that a state can only have a single
\varepsilon
-transition and hence the execution prefix
w\tau
can be extended in the same way as
w
is extended to
w^{\prime}
to obtain
w^{\prime\prime}
for
w\tau
.
Since
w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}
only differ by one
\tau
,
w^{\prime\prime}\sim w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
and the claim follows by transitivity of
\sim
.

Second
, suppose that
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
.

Claim 1.
It holds that
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.

Proof of Claim 1.

We know that
\operatorname{trace}(\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

and
\operatorname{trace}(\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
.
By definition of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s common actions always happen in pairs of sending and receiving a message.
Since there is no out-of-order execution, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
cannot have received
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
yet.
Hence,
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.

End Proof of Claim 1.

For every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we define
\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})=\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.
We know that
(wx){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
so the conditions on the run mapping is satisfied for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

For
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we extend
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
to be maximal, i.e., extending the run further would render one of the conditions for run mappings unsatisfied, and obtain a set of possible extended runs.
For every run, either the last or second-last state of these runs corresponds to some syntactic subterm of
\mathbf{G}
.
Note that one of them will be a prefix of the sender’s run
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.
In case of empty loop branches, the set of runs might be infinite, however, only one is a prefix of the sender’s run.
We denote its syntactic subterm by
G^{\prime}
and we will show that only the option taken by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
can be pursued.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can receive
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, we know that the current local type
L
of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is the result of (merging) the projection of some type(s) of shape
\Sigma_{\_}\_\!\to\!\_\!:\!\_.\_
and all these syntactic subterms are of this form.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can receive
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, the local type
L
must be the result of some merge applying the receiving rule last to compute

\langle L,\_\rangle=\langle L₁,\mathit{Msg}_{1}\rangle\sqcap\ldots\sqcap\langle L_{n},\mathit{Msg}_{n}\rangle

for some
n
and

L_{l}=\ExtCh_{i∈I_{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{l}?m_{i}.AL_{l,i}
for every
l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and for some
l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, it holds that
\langle L_{l},\mathit{Msg}_{l}\rangle=G^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
The other local types correspond to the branches
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
explores concurrently while processing
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Note that we unrolled the binary definition of merge but since
\sqcap
is associative and commutative, we know that
\langle L_{l},\mathit{Msg}_{l}\rangle\sqcap\langle L_{l^{\prime}},\mathit{Msg}_{l^{\prime}}\rangle
is defined for all
l,l^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Therefore it holds, for any
l,l^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, that

\displaystyle∀i∈I_{l}\setminus I_{l^{\prime}}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{l}?m_{i}\notin\mathit{Msg}_{l^{\prime}},

and

\displaystyle∀i∈I_{l^{\prime}}\setminus I_{l}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{l^{\prime}}?m_{i}\notin\mathit{Msg}_{l}.

W.l.o.g., let
k\in\bigcup_{1\leq l\leq n}I_{l}
be the index for which
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{k}
and
m=m_{k}
.
Therefore, for any
l
with
k\notin I_{l}
, it holds that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{k}?m_{k}\notin\mathit{Msg}_{l}
.

Let
l
be some index.
To obtain every individual availability annotated local type
\langle L_{l},\mathit{Msg}_{l}\rangle
, the following case of the projection has been applied:

\langle L_{l},\mathit{Msg}_{l}\rangle=\langle\ExtCh_{j\in J_{l}}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{l}?m_{j}.(G_{j}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}),\bigcup_{j∈J}\operatorname{avail}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\},∅,G_{j})\rangle

for some index sets
J_{l}
and global syntactic subterms
G_{j}
.

From
Lemma

37
, we have that, for every
l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
j\in I_{l}
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, it holds that

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G_{j}\ldots)}\subseteq\operatorname{avail}(\mathcal{B},∅,G_{j})
(*). Recall that
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G_{j}\ldots)}
is defined as all messages that can be sent in any run starting with branch
G_{j}
when no process in
\mathcal{B}
can take any further step.

By definition of
\sqcap
, all the first actions
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{k}?m_{k}
will be merged together to one branch in the local type
L
so
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
will continue with this branch of
L
.
Let us denote this branch by
B_{k}
for now.
By instantiating (*) with
\mathcal{B}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}
whose next action is to receive from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, we know that
m_{k}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{k}
cannot occur in the channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\rangle
in any branch of
L
different from
B_{k}
so
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
cannot diverge from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s run
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
by receiving
m_{k}
out of order.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
follows the run of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
By assumption
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can receive from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
must have taken one of the runs that corresponds to this branch of
L
so we can choose
\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
such that
\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
which proves the claim.

Third
, suppose that
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.

We collect the processes with longer runs than
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in a set:
\mathcal{S}\coloneq\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\mid\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})\land\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\neq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})\}
.
So
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\notin\mathcal{S}
.
We assume
\mathcal{S}
is not empty as the claim follows trivially if it is.
(We could simply extend
\rho
, keep the mapping for all other processes and extend it for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
accordingly.)

As before, for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we extend
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
to be maximal, i.e., extending the run further would render one of the conditions unsatisfied, and obtain a set of possible runs.
In the presence of empty loop branches, this set could be infinite. For this single step of the induction, it suffices to obtain some valid run mapping. By construction, all possible paths through the loop are present. For further extensions of the run mapping, we exploit the idea of prophecy variables to get the run mapping where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
chose the path in the loop as chosen by the deciding processes and show that this does not impose any restrictions.
For every run in this set, either the last or second-last state of these runs corresponds to some syntactic subterm of
\mathbf{G}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can send
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, we know that the current local type
L
of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is the result of (merging) the projection of some type(s) of shape
\Sigma_{\_}\_\!\to\!\_\!:\!\_.\_
and all these syntactic subterms are of this form.

The current local type
L
of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is the result of merging of this shape since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends at this step:

L=\Merge_{i\in I}(\IntCh_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}!m_{j}.(G_{(i,j)}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}))=\IntCh_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}!m_{j}.\Merge_{i\in I}(G_{(i,j)}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})

for some index sets
I
and
J
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}
as well as
m_{j}
and
G_{(i,j)}
for every
j\in J
and
i\in I
.

So the merge
\sqcap
ensures that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has the same options to send after processing
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, no matter which run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
was pursued.
This ensures that we are able to adapt the mapping from the induction hypothesis such that
\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}))=(wx){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.

Inspired by prophecy variables
[
2
]
, we assume that each process
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
in
\mathcal{S}
followed the run that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
will choose to take, i.e.,
\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.
Assuming this, we can re-use the same mapping for them:
\rho^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})=\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
and the overall claim follows.

It remains to show that the mappings
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{S}
can be chosen in such a way without prohibiting any of the processes to proceed with some events.
We will do so by applying
Lemma

36
.

Process
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
might be pursuing more than one run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
Since all first send options for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
could be merged, we know that they are the same over all possible continuations.
Therefore, we show that no matter which option
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
chooses, no process
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{S}
can have processed an action that is incompatible with this branch yet, provided that neither the sender
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
nor each receiver
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
has processed some event in the continuations of the branch of option
i
.

For this, we show for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{S},i\in I
and
j_{1},j_{2}\in J
that

L^{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\}}_{(G_{(i,j_{1})}\ldots)}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=L^{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}\}}_{(G_{(i,j_{2})}\ldots)}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}.

Since we do only compare runs that start at the same state, i.e., the syntactic subterm
\IntCh_{j\in J}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j}!m_{j}.G_{(i,j)}
,
this claim follows from
Lemma

36
.
It suffices to compare those since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can not and does not dictate which of the branches of
I
were taken, but the ones in
J
.
It might happen that some choice wrt. the options in
I
has already been committed but
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
does not know about this at this stage yet.
Still, all processes in
\mathcal{S}
have the same options along all the options in
J
and therefore the use of prophecy variables does not prohibit them from proceeding with any action that is possible in the execution.

∎

Runs Following a Common Path are Extendable

Lemma 38

(Runs following a common path are extendable)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type,

w
an execution prefix of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, and

\rho
a finite run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Then, there is an extension
w^{\prime}
of
w
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.

Proof.

From
Lemma

25
, we know that every prefix of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
has some run in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
It holds that
\operatorname{trace}(\rho)\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
so there is a run for
\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
With
Lemma

22
, it follows that every trace in
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\operatorname{trace}(\rho))
has a run in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that
w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\operatorname{trace}(\rho))
.
To prove this, we need to find
w^{\prime}
such that
w\leq w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.

By
Lemma

23
, it suffices to find a channel-compliant
w^{\prime}
such that

w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.

For every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, there is
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
such that
(w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}).y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
We show that there is a channel-compliant
w^{\prime}=wy
such that
y{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Since
w
is an execution prefix of a CSM,
w
is channel-compliant (
Lemma

20
).
We need to construct
y
and start with
y=\varepsilon
.
We extend
y
by consuming prefixes of
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and do so as long as some
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is not empty.
At all times, we preserve the following invariant for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
:

(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.

If there is any
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m).y^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we extend
y=y.({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m)
and set
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=y^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
This preserves the invariant and appending actions of shape
\_\triangleright\_!\_
to
y
does preserve channel-compliancy of
wy
.
If there is no such
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
(and not all
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
are empty), there is a set of roles
\mathcal{S}
such that
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft\_?\_).y^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{S}
.
We define the shortest prefix
\rho^{\prime}
of
\rho
such that there is some role whose trace is included in the projection of
\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime})
:

\rho^{\prime}\coloneq\min_{\leq}\{\rho^{\prime}\mid\exists{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{S}.\,(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\text{ for }\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho\}.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime\prime}.q.q^{\prime}
.
By design, we know that the label
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for transition
(q,x,q^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
Therefore, the choice of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is unique and for all other roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, it holds that
(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\neq\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
.
By assumption, it holds that
(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\leq\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
and hence
\operatorname{trace}(\rho^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\leq(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
because
\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
(*).

Let
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m).y^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
and
m
.
We claim that appending the first action
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
from
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
y
, i.e.,
y\coloneq y.({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m)
and
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=y^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, preserves channel-compliancy of
wy
.
It is straightforward that the invariant is preserved.
For channel-compliancy of
wy
, we need to show that

\mathcal{V}((wy){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_})\leq\mathcal{V}((wy){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_}).

By the invariant, we know that

(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\text{ and }(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,
so it holds that

(w.y.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_}\text{ and }(w.y.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_}.

Because global types describe protocols,

\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
is channel-compliant which yields that

\mathcal{V}(\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_})=\mathcal{V}(\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_})
.
This ensures that the next message in
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\rangle
is
m
if it exists.
By design of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, the send and reception part of a
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\!:\!m
always occur in pairs on the run
\rho
, i.e., it holds that for any transitions
q^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m}q^{\prime\prime}
in
\rho
, there is
q
such that
q\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m}q^{\prime}
in
\rho
.
From (*), we know that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m
must be the next action of shape
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_
with unmatched reception in
wy
.
Since our channels preserve FIFO order, the next message in channel
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\rangle
is
m
.

Overall, this procedure ensures that we can always extend
wy
by appending prefixes of
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in a way that preserves channel-compliancy of
wy
and

(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Note that
\rho
is finite and hence each
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is finite so the procedure terminates.
For
w^{\prime}
, we can choose the result of the procedure
wy
and it holds, for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, that

(wy){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

which proves the claim.
∎

From Run Mapping via Control Flow Agreement to Protocol Fidelity

To show protocol fidelity, we use a strengthening of the run mappings by a progress condition.
Any execution prefix can be extended to match all actions in the run given by the run mapping.

Definition 39

(Control Flow Agreement)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type.
A CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

satisfies
Control Flow Agreement (CFA)
for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
iff
for every execution prefix
w
of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
,
there is a run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that the following holds:

•

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is a prefix of

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and

•

w
can be extended (in
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
) to
w^{\prime}
such that

w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}_{\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})}(\rho)
.

Lemma 40

(Run mappings entail CFA)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type,
If
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a family of run mappings for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,
then
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies CFA for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.

Proof.

Let
w
be an execution prefix of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
with witness run
\rho
and its run mapping
\rho(\hbox{-})
.
We can assume that
\rho
is finite since
w
is a finite execution prefix.
We know that

\operatorname{trace}(\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
and
\rho({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\leq\rho
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Hence, it holds that

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
which is exactly the first condition of CFA.

The second condition requires that we can extend
w
to
w^{\prime}
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

such that
w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
Since
\rho
is finite, we can apply
Lemma

38
and obtain an extension
w^{\prime}
of
w
in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
w^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
∎

Lemma 41

(CFA entails protocol fidelity)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type.
If the CSM
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies CFA for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,
then

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))=\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}).

Proof.

First,
Lemma

25
yields that

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}).

The language of a CSM is closed under
\sim
(
Lemma

22
), hence the first inclusion holds.

It remains to prove the second inclusion:

\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})).

Let
w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.
For both the finite and infinite case, we will use
Definition

39
to obtain a maximal run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
for which
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

We do a case split whether
w
is finite or infinite.

In case
w
is finite, we use
Definition

39
to find the corresponding run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
and want to show that
w\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
So we do not extend
w
to obtain
w^{\prime}
but keep it as it is.
First, we apply
Definition

39
to obtain the shortest run
\rho
that satisfies both conditions.

We show that
\rho
ends in a final state
s
and is hence maximal in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
Let
(q,\xi)
be the final configuration of
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
that has been reached with trace
w
and therefore
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be the states for the individual state machines
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Since
(q,\xi)
is final, every
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Then, we know that
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
corresponds to
0
by definition of
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
By
Proposition

24
,
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
does not have any outgoing transitions.

Proposition

15
states that every run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
is also possible in
\mathsf{LAut}(G{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Therefore, by contradiction, it follows that a run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
with trace
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
cannot be extended to a run with a longer trace for all
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Hence, the run of
\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
cannot be extended to a run with a longer trace either, i.e., there can only be
\varepsilon
-transitions.
By definition of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,
\varepsilon
-transitions occur from states corresponding to
t
and
\mu t.G^{\prime}
.
We claim that
s
does not correspond to any
t
or syntactic subterm of shape
\mu t.G^{\prime}
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that
s
corresponds to some
t
or subterm of shape
\mu t.G^{\prime}
.
Global types have guarded recursion, i.e.,
G^{\prime}
cannot be
0
for any
\mu t.G^{\prime}
.
Then there is some non-
\varepsilon
-transition from
s
which is a contradiction.

Hence, there is also no
\varepsilon
-transition from
s
and
s
does not have any outgoing transitions.
By
Proposition

24
,
s
corresponds to
0
, is final in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, and
\rho
is maximal in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.

It remains to show that we do not need to extend
w
to
w^{\prime}
for any role.
We define
C\coloneq\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\mid w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}
.
We claim that
C=\emptyset
.
Towards a contradiction, let us assume that
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in C
. Since we reached the final state
s
with
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
which corresponds to
0
, there is no successor in
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
for
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
either and there is no possibility to extend
w
for any
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
but this contradicts the second condition of CFA and we do not need to extend
w^{\prime}
for any role.

So it holds that
w\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
Because
\rho
is maximal, we have that
\operatorname{trace}(\rho)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
and the claim (for finite
w
) follows because
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
is closed under
\sim
by definition.

Suppose that
w
is infinite.
We show that there is an infinite run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that matches with
w
when closing with
\sim
.

From
Definition

39
, we can obtain a finite run
\rho
for every prefix
u
of
w
such that the conditions for CFA hold, i.e., there is an extension
u^{\prime}
of
u
such that
u^{\prime}\sim\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
To simplify the argument, we use an idea similar to prophecy variables
[
2
]
as in the proof of
Lemma

27
.
Here, we can use a similar oracle that tells which witness run and mapping to use for every prefix of
w
.
This does not restrict the roles in any way.
All conditions of CFA hold for the prefix and this witness run since they hold for longer prefixes of
w
.
By this, we ensure that the run
\rho
can always be extended for longer prefixes of
w

(\square)
.

Consider a tree
\mathcal{T}
where each node corresponds to a run
\rho
on some finite prefix
w^{\prime}\leq w
.
The root is labelled by the empty run. The children of a node
\rho
are runs that extend
\rho
by a single transition—these exist by
(\square)
.
Since our CSM, derived from a global type, is a finite number of finite state machines,
\mathcal{T}
is finitely branching.
By König’s Lemma, there is an infinite path in
\mathcal{T}
that corresponds to an infinite run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
for which
w\preceq^{\omega}_{\sim}\operatorname{trace}(\rho)
.
By definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
for infinite traces, this yields
w\in\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
∎

Lemma 42

(CFA for
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
entails deadlock freedom)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a projectable global type.
If
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies CFA for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,
then
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock free.

Proof.

Towards a contradiction, assume that
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a deadlock.
Then, there is an execution prefix
w
for which
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
reaches a non-final configuration
(q,\xi)
and no transition is possible.
Let
\rho
be the maximal run from
Definition

39
for which the conditions of CFA hold.
Since
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
cannot make another step, the second condition can only apply for
w^{\prime}=w
, so all events along
\rho
have been processed by all roles, i.e.,
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
by
Lemma

23
.

Claim I:

We claim the last state
s
of
\rho
corresponds to
0
.

Proof of Claim I.

Towards a contradiction, assume that
\rho
can be extended, i.e.,
s
has a successor.
Since
\rho
is maximal, there cannot be any
\varepsilon
-transitions from
s
but only some transition labelled different than
\varepsilon
.
Since all roles have processed all events along
\rho
, such a transition can only be labelled by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, and
m
.
Hence, there is a run for
(w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}).{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
By
Proposition

15
, there is a run for
(w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}).{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can hence take another transition which contradicts the assumption that
(q,\xi)
is a deadlock.

End Proof of Claim I.

So we know that the last state
s
of
\rho
corresponds to
0
.
Again, by
Proposition

15
, there is no transition for any
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
from
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
because
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
has no transition for any
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
All channels in
\xi
are empty and
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\operatorname{trace}(\rho){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
By
Proposition

24
, we know that there is only a single state in
\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
that does not have outgoing transitions, i.e., the one that corresponds to
0
and hence is final.
But then, all individual final states
q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
are final (and all channels in
\xi
are empty) so
(q,\xi)
is a final configuration which yields the desired contradiction.
∎

C.3
Proof of

Theorem

16
:

Any Projectable Global Type is Implementable

Now,
we can prove

Theorem

16
:
Any projectable global type is implementable.

Proof of
Theorem

16
.

Let
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the CSM of local types.
Then, we know that
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has a family of run mappings for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
by
Lemma

27
.
This, in turn, entails that
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
satisfies control-flow agreement for
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
by
Lemma

40
.
This satisfies the condition for
Lemma

42
(and
41
) and hence
\{\!\!\{{\mathsf{LAut}(\mathbf{G}{\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock free.
With
Lemmas

25
and
41
, protocol fidelity holds and the claim follows.
∎
